These annotations warrant further investigation and validation processes, as they may harbour other, potentially new, bioactive molecules that could not be discussed in our result interpretation.</p>
<p>Importantly, the identification of these compounds was achieved through the combination of a relatively high‐throughput and relatively low‐cost metabolite extraction procedure, using as little as 60 mL ethyl acetate per strain, and of advanced community‐developed computational annotation workflows. This approach stands in contrast to traditional methods requiring extensive extraction of bacteria over longer periods, larger volumes of solvents and subsequent chromatography separation and analytical chemistry tools.</p></section></section><section id="mbt270269-sec-0017"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.4. Validating the Annotation of Some Putatively Detected Compounds</h3>
<p>Since the metabolomic analysis performed yields putative annotations derived from computational workflows and existing database knowledge, additional experimental validation is required to confirm the identification of each compound. One straightforward approach for validation is comparison with commercially available standards. Accordingly, we assessed the annotation of 11 commercially available pure standards. Detection and chemical data for these compounds are provided in Table <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0008" class="usa-link">S7</a>. All purchased compound standards were purified from streptomycetes except for myriocin, which is known to be produced by <em>Streptomyces</em> and other organisms but is commercially available from the fungus <em>M. sterilia</em>.</p>
<p>The annotation of eight out of the 11 compounds was confirmed through LC–MS analysis of the pure standards, compared to the putative compound peaks in the extracts of their respective producing strains (blue circles in Figure <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0001" class="usa-link">S7</a>). These compounds were <a href="https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/CH/de/substance/actinomycind12554250760?utm_source=google&amp;utm_medium=cpc&amp;utm_campaign=18067822289&amp;utm_content=140588849496&amp;gclid=Cj0KCQjwzZmwBhD8ARIsAH4v1gXdpqdrbBd0r_HI5KhOqfwfYzrSlRrzkxPbc7tGlPZ93sP1ppZixUwaAiZoEALw_wcB" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">actinomycin D</a>, <a href="https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/CH/fr/search/1397-94-0?focus=products&amp;gclid=Cj0KCQiAtaOtBhCwARIsAN_x-3LgY-MLU_H-FnWX9TWZ_BdDodIiKp4eS2hhrNP2wo5028Boa7dBLYQaAo28EALw_wcB&amp;page=1&amp;perpage=30&amp;sort=relevance&amp;term=1397-94-0&amp;type=cas_number&amp;utm_campaign=18061529913&amp;utm_content=135373896370&amp;utm_medium=cpc&amp;utm_source=google" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">antimycin A</a>, <a href="https://www.caymanchem.com/product/15386" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">ikarugamycin</a>, <a href="https://www.chemie-brunschwig.ch/de/shop/?term=Indole-3-carboxylic+acid&amp;brand=" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">indole‐3‐carboxylic acid</a>, <a href="https://www.chemie-brunschwig.ch/de/shop/?term=Ochromycinone&amp;brand=" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">ochromycinone</a>, <a href="https://adipogen.com/bvt-0446-piperafizine-b.html/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">piperafizine B</a>, <a href="https://adipogen.com/bvt-0036-resistomycin.html/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">resistomycin</a> and <a href="https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/CH/de/search/2001-95-8?focus=products&amp;gclid=Cj0KCQjwzZmwBhD8ARIsAH4v1gVUwF34hg_j-JyveoUmBjXeEYB6YB8jF1WaBzhwv6nIKuu1r0ysNM4aAo_8EALw_wcB&amp;page=1&amp;perpage=30&amp;sort=relevance&amp;term=2001-95-8&amp;type=cas_number&amp;utm_campaign=18061529913&amp;utm_content=135373885570&amp;utm_medium=cpc&amp;utm_source=google" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">valinomycin</a>. <a href="https://www.caymanchem.com/product/22225" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Macbecin I</a> and <a href="https://www.caymanchem.com/product/63150" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">myriocin</a> (orange circles in Figure <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0001" class="usa-link">S7</a>) were detected with different adducts and retention times, suggesting that these strains might have produced different isomers of these standard compounds. This assumption, however, requires further investigation. The antimycin A standard consists of four components: antimycin A1, A2, A3 and A4, which were all detected in the strain extract (Figure <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0001" class="usa-link">S7</a>).</p></section><section id="mbt270269-sec-0018"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5. Assessing the Activity of Selected Compounds Against Two 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 Developmental Stages</h3>
<p>In addition to annotation validation, we aimed to assess the contribution of each annotated compound to its strain's activity against 
<em>P. infestans</em>
, as well as to determine the concentration required to induce the observed inhibition. Therefore, we evaluated the activity of the 11 purchased compounds against two developmental stages of 
<em>P. infestans</em>
. <a href="https://store.apolloscientific.co.uk/search?search=borrelidin" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Borrelidin</a> activity against 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 mycelial growth was evaluated in a previous study (Gillon et al. <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0034" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0034">2023</a>).</p>
<p>The activity of the tested compounds on 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 mycelial growth was assessed using a disk diffusion method with varying concentrations. Antimycin A was the most active compound against 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 with a half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC<sub>50</sub>) value of 64.7 μg, followed by actinomycin D (IC<sub>50</sub> = 81.9 μg), macbecin I (IC<sub>50</sub> = 105.4 μg), myriocin (IC<sub>50</sub> = 518.7 μg) and a very slight activity was exhibited by ikarugamycin (Figure <a href="#mbt270269-fig-0004" class="usa-link">4</a>). In contrast, the other six compounds were inactive at the tested concentrations, as shown in Figure <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0001" class="usa-link">S8</a>. The inhibitory activity of antimycin A, actinomycin D, macbecin I and myriocin increased with higher concentrations, indicating a dose‐dependent effect, consistent with previous observations for borrelidin, although borrelidin exhibited significantly stronger activity against 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 mycelial growth with an IC<sub>50</sub> value of only 0.99 μg.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="mbt270269-fig-0004"><h4 class="obj_head">FIGURE 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/068d/12663769/1a388502c9bd/MBT2-18-e70269-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-9" height="540" width="709" alt="FIGURE 4"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/mbt270269-fig-0004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Representative plates showing the growth inhibition of 
<em>Phytophthora infestans</em>
 in the presence of different amounts of the pure compounds actinomycin D, antimycin A, macbecin I, myriocin and ikarugamycin. The left disks are methanol blanks, and the right disks are the different concentrations tested. The experiment was performed once with two technical replicates.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Beyond mycelial growth inhibition, antimycin A, ikarugamycin and borrelidin compounds exhibited remarkable activity against zoospore germination, an epidemiologically relevant developmental stage of 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 (Tani and Judelson <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0091" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0091">2006</a>; Kasteel et al. <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0042" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0042">2023</a>). These three compounds inhibited the zoospore germination with IC<sub>50</sub> values below the tested concentration range (&lt; 0.5 μg/mL), as shown in Figure <a href="#mbt270269-fig-0005" class="usa-link">5</a> and Table <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0008" class="usa-link">S7</a>. The remaining compounds showed varying degrees of inhibition on germ tube length, with IC<sub>50</sub> values ranging from 5.17 μg/mL (myriocin) to 14.44 μg/mL (acetaminophen) (Figure <a href="#mbt270269-fig-0005" class="usa-link">5</a>, Table <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0008" class="usa-link">S7</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="mbt270269-fig-0005"><h4 class="obj_head">FIGURE 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/068d/12663769/a6abbd1e79fa/MBT2-18-e70269-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-11" height="594" width="709" alt="FIGURE 5"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/mbt270269-fig-0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Inhibition activity of varying concentrations of standard compounds on 
<em>Phytophthora infestans</em>
 zoospore germination. (A) Representative pictures showing the inhibition of 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 zoospore germination in the presence of different concentrations of pure compounds. (B) Box plot depicting the percentage of 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 germ tube length relative to the water control for each standard compound at four different concentrations. Different letters indicate significant differences between treatments, as determined by a two‐way analysis of variance followed by Tukey's multiple comparisons test (<em>p</em> &lt; 0.05).</p></figcaption></figure><p>It is worth noting that some compounds demonstrated differing levels of activity across the two developmental stages, suggesting distinct modes of action. However, these differences may also result from the distinct experimental setups, solid media versus liquid media, which affect compound diffusion rates. For example, ikarugamycin exhibited weak inhibition of mycelial growth, with only slight inhibition near the filter paper disk (Figure <a href="#mbt270269-fig-0004" class="usa-link">4</a>), but strongly inhibited zoospore germination in the 96‐well plate assay (Figure <a href="#mbt270269-fig-0005" class="usa-link">5</a>).</p>
<p>Several of the validated compounds have previously been reported to exhibit activity against various fungal pathogens. For instance, actinomycin D has demonstrated antifungal effects against <em>B. cinerea</em>, <em>F. oxysporum</em> and <em>V. dahliae</em> (Toumatia et al. <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0093" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0093">2015</a>; Zeng et al. <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0109">2019</a>; Yong et al. <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0108" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0108">2022</a>), antimycin A against <em>R. solani</em> and <em>M. oryzae</em>, and ikarugamycin derivatives against 
<em>Aspergillus fumigatus</em>
 and 
<em>C. albicans</em>
 (Lacret et al. <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0050">2015</a>). Similarly, myriocin has been reported to inhibit <em>Fusarium graminearum</em> (Shao et al. <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0085" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0085">2021</a>), resistomycin against <em>Valsa mali</em>, <em>Magnaporthe grisea</em> (rice blast) and <em>Pyricularia grisea</em> (Zhang et al. <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0112" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0112">2013</a>), and valinomycin against <em>B. cinerea</em> (Park et al. <a href="#mbt270269-bib-0071" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="mbt270269-bib-0071">2008</a>). However, to the best of our knowledge, this study is the first to demonstrate the potent activity of these compounds against 
<em>P. infestans</em>
, highlighting their potential significance for the control of late blight disease.</p>
<p>Beyond validating the potent anti‐
<em>P. infestans</em>
 activity of these compounds, whose activity could be validated using pure standards (in bold in Table <a href="#mbt270269-tbl-0001" class="usa-link">1</a>), this study also highlights a significant set of 75 candidate molecules with potential applicability for managing late blight disease caused by the oomycete 
<em>P. infestans</em>
. These candidate compounds, along with their associated phenotypes, are summarised in Table <a href="#mbt270269-tbl-0001" class="usa-link">1</a>. Although some of the validated compounds exhibited varying levels of activity against mycelial growth (six active compounds) and zoospore germination (11 significantly different from the control), this represents a relatively small number compared to the overall set of candidate molecules. Therefore, the activity of non‐purchasable compound standards should be further validated following their purification or synthesis. Notably, several unvalidated compounds exhibit strong potential for anti‐
<em>P. infestans</em>
 activity based on their consistent detection across multiple highly active extracts or 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 morphology alteration categories. For example, 2‐hydroxyethyl clavam was uniquely detected in both the genome and metabolome of the two most active <em>Streptomyces</em> strains, B108 and B113. Albonoursin was identified in both the genome and metabolome of the highly active 
<em>S. albus</em>
 strain B19, as well as in two 
<em>N. dassonvillei</em>
 strains, B67 and B114 (morphology one). Additionally, naphthyridinomycin was detected in both the genome and metabolome of strains associated with morphologies one and four. Further investigations are warranted to confirm the structural annotation and evaluate the bioactivity of these compounds through both in vitro and <em>in planta</em> assays.</p>

<div class="tw-foot p">
<div class="fn" id="mbt270269-note-0001"><p>
<em>Note:</em> Compounds in bold were tested as commercial standards.</p></div>
<div class="fn" id="mbt270269-note-0002"><p>Abbreviations: A, active extract; BGC, biosynthetic gene cluster detected in the genome; M, morphology alteration induced by the strains; VA, very active extract.</p></div>
<div class="fn" id="mbt270269-note-0003">
<sup>
<sup>a</sup>
</sup><p class="display-inline">Compounds known to be produced by actinobacteria.</p>
</div>
</div></section></section><section id="mbt270269-sec-0019"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.6. Sixty‐Eight Biosynthetic Gene Clusters Were Associated With Active Strains</h3>
<p>Beyond the compounds detected in our metabolomic analysis, we analysed the genomes of our strains to identify BGCs encoding bioactive compounds that might not have been produced or successfully extracted. Beyond validating our metabolite annotations, analysing the strains' genomes might also reveal other metabolites associated with active strains but not detected with our metabolomic analysis. Leveraging our large number of samples, we aimed to exclude many biosynthetic gene clusters that are also present in inactive strains, facilitating the identification of distinct clusters of metabolites carried in the genomes of active strains. Around 300 BGCs were detected across the sequenced genomes (Table <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0009" class="usa-link">S8</a>: Sheet 1). Of these, 68 BGCs were found in the genomes of active strains, showing more than 50% similarity, as illustrated in Table <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0009" class="usa-link">S8</a>: Sheet 2.</p>
<p>Fourteen of these BGCs corresponded to compounds that were identified in the metabolomes of their respective producing strains, validating the chemical annotations: 2‐hydroxyethyl clavam, 4‐Z‐annimycin, 7‐prenylisatin, actinomycin D, albonoursin, antimycin, borrelidin, collinomycin, dudomycin A, ikarugamycin, naphthyridinomycin, resistomycin, streptophenazine and xantholipin (Table <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0009" class="usa-link">S8</a>: Sheet 3).</p>
<p>Although we focused on BGCs with relatively high similarity, we cannot exclude the possibility that some BGCs with lower similarity also encode their respective compounds. For example, we detected and annotated valinomycin in the metabolome of strain B171, even though the corresponding BGC in the strain's genome had only 17% similarity to the valinomycin BGC in the database.</p>
<p>Based on the findings of this work, the next crucial steps involve validating as many potential active compounds as possible and assessing their activity against different developmental stages of 
<em>P. infestans</em>
 grown in vitro and in planta. Validation of annotations could be achieved by purchasing commercially available compounds, synthesising non‐commercially available ones, or isolating and purifying these compounds to elucidate their structure using NMR. Additionally, characterising biosynthetic gene clusters associated with active strains and examining their expression on different media is a possible approach to exploit the potential of these strains. Subsequently, once the bioactive compounds are identified, the next step would be to characterise their potential toxicity on plants and animal models, as ensuring safety in developing biocontrol agents is crucial. Compound activity and cytotoxicity would serve as prioritising criteria for further investigations. Moreover, considering each compound's mode of action, if reported, or investigating it if yet unreported, is essential for prioritising compounds suitable for further application as anti‐
<em>P. infestans</em>
 treatments, as compounds with specific, targeted activity would be expected to have less impact on non‐target organisms.</p>
<p>Additionally, the compounds identified in this study may hold potential as biocontrol agents beyond 
<em>P. infestans</em>
, warranting further investigation against a broader spectrum of plant pathogens. To support such efforts, the associated metabolomic and genomic datasets generated here can be repurposed to pinpoint compounds exhibiting selective or enhanced activity against other agriculturally relevant pathogens.</p></section></section><section id="mbt270269-sec-0020"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Conclusion</h2>
<p>In conclusion, this study establishes that a multi‐omics approach applied to phylogenetically related bacterial strains exhibiting differential bioactivity is a robust strategy for discovering biologically relevant metabolites. By leveraging the expectation that closely related strains share a common metabolite background, this strategy served as a practical shortcut for pinpointing the most promising active metabolites against our target pathogen. Coupled with a streamlined metabolite extraction protocol, high‐resolution mass spectrometry, and advanced computational tools for metabolite annotation and biosynthetic prediction, this framework enabled the identification of over 75 candidate compounds with potential activity against 
<em>P. infestans</em>
. Importantly, this integrative methodology is broadly applicable for elucidating bioactive metabolite profiles associated with any phenotypic variation among closely related microbial strains.</p></section><section id="mbt270269-sec-1002"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Author Contributions</h2>

<p>All data required for this study is included in this paper, its <a href="#mbt270269-supitem-0001" class="usa-link">Supporting Information</a> and through the provided hyperlinks. The full spectral data set corresponding to the extract collection has been uploaded to the MassIVE repository under the following MassIVE id <a href="https://massive.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/private-dataset.jsp?task=189f4bfc21b94a87924a81671f14b0fe" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">MSV000095751</a>. The genome sequences of the 59 actinomycete strains can be accessed in the European Nucleotide Archive under the project accession numbers <a href="https://www.ebi.ac.uk/ena/browser/view/PRJEB59914" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PRJEB59914</a> and <a href="https://www.ebi.ac.uk/ena/browser/view/PRJEB67372" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PRJEB67372</a>.</p></section></section></section><footer class="p courtesy-note font-secondary font-sm text-center"><hr class="headless">
<p>Articles from Microbial Biotechnology are provided here courtesy of <strong>Wiley</strong></p></footer></section></article>
